===== PAGE 1 =====
Quantum Mechanics on Non-Hausdorff One-Manifolds
with Finite Graph Resolutions:
Analytic Completion, Branching,
and Invariant-Sector Scattering
Abhiram Sripat
Florence Quantum Labs
abhiram@florencequantumlabs.com
Abstract.We develop quantum mechanics on a finite graph-resolved class of second-countable,T1,
non-Hausdorff one-manifolds by separating local differential expressions from the global extension
and completion data that determine closed operator domains. For binary adjunctions, transported-
jet equalisers give the formal compatibility conditions, while Whitney realisability supplies the
residual finite-order obstruction; exponentially tangent branches show that formal compatibility
alone can fail. Under explicit trace hypotheses, Sobolev closures are determined by the realisable
incidence traces in the noncritical rangej < m−c/p. For finite smooth graph resolutions this
yields exact integer-order closures, formal endpoint-jet equalisers, signed return holonomy, and
finite prolongation profiles.
At first order, scalar completion gives the continuousH1-domain on the Hausdorff propagation
graph. A marked resolving-presentation measure pushes forward to edge weights and the flat
kinetic form gives the weighted Kirchhoff Hamiltonian. Multiple-origin lines and circles, branching
junctions, finite trees, and split-and-rejoin networks provide explicit deficiency indices, bright–dark
decompositions, transmission criteria, and compact dark states. For a rank-r Hermitian bundle
on the line withk origins, relative transitionsW2,...,W k∈U (r)select the transmitting space
VW =⋂k
a=2 ker(Wa−I ). Compact-group representations identify this with the invariant sector of
the generated subgroup; connected compact semisimple groups require at most two relative gluing
matrices for full invariant completion, and low-degreeSU (3)tensors give explicit optimal ranks
and a quartic rank-two invariant projector. The resulting hierarchy distinguishes weighted-graph
data, bundle-selected trace domains, cycle holonomy, and higher transported jets.
Keywords:non-Hausdorff manifolds, quantum mechanics, vector bundles, section extension, trans-
ported jets, Whitney extension, Sobolev closure, quantum graphs, self-adjoint operators, branching,
holonomy, compact groups, invariant-sector scattering.

===== PAGE 7 =====
Part I
Analytic foundations and completed domains
1 Introduction
By a non-Hausdorff one-manifold we mean a second-countable,T1, locally Euclidean smooth space
without the Hausdorff axiom. Such spaces occur in adjunction and leaf-space constructions (Haefliger
and Reeb 1957, O’Connell 2023, 2024, Lysynskyi and Maksymenko 2026a,b, Vlasenko and Maksymenko
2026) and have also appeared in mathematical-physics models (Heller et al. 2011).
We study finite smooth graph resolutionsπ : M→ Γwith finite metric Hausdorff reflectionΓ.
Metric-measure models use a marked Hausdorff presentationq : ~M→M , an edge metric, and a positive
presentation measure~µ, inducingµΓ = (π◦q )∗~µ. The multiplicities entering the pushed-forward measure
belong to this marked presentation; bundle transport, connections, and quadratic forms are additional
quantum data. The class includes multiple-origin lines and circles, finite split sets, branching trees, and
split-and-rejoin systems.
On the line with two origins, Hausdorff-valued scalars factor throughR, whereas a relative-sign
rank-one bundle forces smooth component representatives to be flat at the origin. Its quantum domain is
therefore determined only after this extension condition is completed in the operator-relevant topology.
For binary adjunctions, component restriction becomes a Hausdorff prolongation problem. Trans-
ported jets give necessary equaliser conditions, but exponentially tangent branches show that full
Whitney realisability is an independent obstruction; finite clean conically tame systems remove it. Under
explicit trace and density hypotheses,Wm,p-closure retains precisely the realisable incidence traces in
the noncritical rangej <m−c/p . Finite graph resolutions sharpen this to exact integer-order endpoint
closures, signed formal holonomy, and prolongation profiles.
At first order the scalar domain isH1
cont(Γ); the pushed-forward marked measure supplies edge
weights, and the flat derivative form yields the weighted Kirchhoff Hamiltonian. The oriented derivative
has its own boundary form and deficiency theory. Branching, trees, and split-and-rejoin geometries then
give explicit channel decompositions, transmission criteria, resonant transparency, and compact dark
states.
For rank-r unitary gluing on the line with k origins, the relative transitions select VW =⋂k
a=2 ker(Wa−I ): the flat Friedrichs operator is free onVW, Dirichlet on its orthogonal complement,
and has transmission projectorPVW. IfWa =ρ(ga), this becomes the invariant sector of the generated
compact subgroup. Connected compact semisimple groups require at most two relative gluing matrices
for invariant completion, with explicit low-degreeSU(3)ranks and a quartic rank-two projector.
The analysis uses standard Whitney extension, Sobolev trace, quadratic-form, self-adjoint extension,
quantum-graph, and compact-group theory (Whitney 1934, Fefferman 2006, Adams and Fournier 2003,
Kato 1995, Kostrykin and Schrader 1999, Berkolaiko and Kuchment 2013, Hall 2015, Fulton and Harris
1991). The structural contribution is the derivation, within the stated non-Hausdorff class, of a single
chain from incidence and bundle transport to extension images, closed trace domains, resolution-induced
weights, and invariant-sector scattering projectors.
Throughout,resolution-inducedmeans determined by the marked analytic resolution together with
its graph metric, presentation measure, stated bundle transport or flat connection, and unperturbed
quadratic form. Topology alone does not determine these choices; other connections, potentials, measures,
defect interactions, or self-adjoint extensions may retain additional information.
Parts I–IV develop the analytic foundations, graph-resolved dynamics, unitary gluing, and detectabil-
ity hierarchy; the appendices contain the cutoff and Sobolev perturbation estimates.
7

===== PAGE 8 =====
2 The line with two origins: the motivating quantum problem
The line with two origins provides the basic contrast: its scalar sector is elementary, whereas the
relative-sign rank-one sector has a nontrivial extension defect. Section 11 returns to the corresponding
closed dynamics after the Sobolev analysis.
2.1 Geometry and scalar calibration
Let
L2 := (R1⊔R 2)/∼,(x,1)∼(x,2)forx̸= 0.
Writeo1 = [0]1, o 2 = [0]2. The Hausdorff reflection isq : L2−→R, q ([x]) = x, q (o1) =
q(o2) = 0.
Proposition 2.1(Hausdorff factorisation).Every continuous map from L2 to a Hausdorff space
identifies the two origins and factors uniquely throughq.
Proof. If the two origins had distinct images, disjoint neighbourhoods of those images would pull back
to disjoint neighbourhoods ofo1 and o2, contradicting their inseparability. Sinceq is the quotient
collapsing only the exceptional fibre, the induced factor map is unique and continuous.
For the identity-glued smooth structure,C∞(L2,C )∼=C∞(R,C ). For the scalar calibration, equip
the Hausdorff reflection with Lebesgue measure. Equivalently, after an irrelevant overall normalization,
one may use any marked resolving presentation whose pushed-forward measure is a positive constant
multiple ofdx. Iff = ˆf◦q , define its scalarL2-norm by∥f∥ 2

Its associated self-adjoint operator is the free Laplacian−d2/dx2 onH 2(R).
Theorem 2.2(Scalar reduction).The doubled origin creates neither an additional scalarL2-degree of
freedom nor a scalar point interaction for the resolution-induced free Hamiltonian.
Proof. Smooth compatibility in the identity-glued atlas identifies the two local representatives with
one function inC∞(R). The two origins have zero mass, so pullback byq is unitary fromL2(R)onto
the scalarL2-space. Under this unitary the flat kinetic form is

self-adjoint operator is the free Laplacian onH2(R).
2.2 The nontrivial rank-one sector
For the standard orientation and flat metric,Spin(1)∼= Z2. We use the usual cocycle definition of a
spin structure (Lawson and Michelsohn 1989): a locally trivial principalSpin(1)-bundle together with
an equivariant fibrewise double covering of the oriented orthonormal frame bundle. All bundles and
bundle maps are taken over the stated non-Hausdorff atlas. SinceSO(1) ={1}, this is equivalently a
lift of the trivial oriented-frame cocycle by locally constant{±1}-valued transitions. This direct cocycle
formulation avoids any appeal to classification theorems requiring a Hausdorff or paracompact base.
The overlap of the two standard charts has two connected components, so a spin transition is
determined by two signsε−, ε +. After a change of local frame, only the relative signη = ε+ε−
remains.
Theorem 2.3(Spin structures on L2).There are exactly two spin-structure isomorphism classes: the
trivial class and the relative-sign class represented by
g(x) =
{
+1, x<0,
−1, x>0.
8

===== PAGE 9 =====
Proof. The transition function is locally constant becauseSpin(1)is discrete, hence is determined by
(ε−,ε +)∈{± 1}2. A change of spin frame on the two connected charts multiplies both signs by the
same relative gauge factor. It can normalizeε− = 1, leaving η = ε+ε−∈{± 1}. The value ofη is
unchanged by every further gauge transformation, and the two displayed normal forms are therefore
inequivalent.
For the relative-sign class, smooth compatibility through the two origins forces every one-sided
derivative to vanish at the exceptional coordinate. Equivalently, restriction to either Hausdorff chart
identifies the smooth global sections with
F∞
0 (R) :=
{
f∈C ∞(R) :f (j)(0) = 0for everyj≥0
}
.
The finite-Ck restriction image is identified in Theorem 3.4. Although the relative-sign bundle is smooth
on the non-Hausdorff atlas, its representative on either Hausdorff chart must prolong through the
missing origin with the prescribed regularity. The resulting component-extension problem is formulated
next for binary adjunctions and finite branch systems; its Sobolev closure will determine the closed
quantum domain used in Part II.
3 Adjunction bundles and failure of unrestricted section extension
Throughout this section,r∈N 0∪{∞}. LetM1 and M2 be smooth Hausdorff manifolds, letUi⊆M i

imply that an arbitrary section on one component admits a compatible section on the other.

Proof. Proposition 3.3 verifies the stated base and bundle-gluing hypotheses. Corollary 3.5 shows that
the constant component sectionu= 1has no global smooth extension.
The obstruction occurs at the closure of the gluing region. In the published proof of (O’Connell
2024, Theorem 2.5), a component section is restricted to the closure of the overlap and the bundle
isomorphism on the open overlap is then used to interpret the transported data as a section over the
corresponding closed subbundle. The colimit-bundle hypotheses specify that bundle isomorphism only
on the open overlap. In the present example its fibre multiplier has one-sided limits1and−1, so no
continuous extension of the bundle transition to the closure exists. The transported constant section
therefore has incompatible one-sided limits and cannot be prolonged.
Remark 3.7(Scope of the correction).The counterexample does not affect the construction of the
colimit bundle, nor the statement that a family of component sections already satisfying the overlap
compatibility equations determines a global section. It concerns the additional surjectivity assertion that
an arbitrary prescribed component section always admits compatible companions. No claim is made here
about later results that use the theorem; the point at issue is only the unrestricted component-surjectivity
step. A sufficient replacement hypothesis for the present paper is stated in Theorem 3.8.
The two one-sidedk-jets forced on the second component areJk

the one-sided jets of a commonCk-germ exactly whenJk

prolongation defect rather than a failure of bundle gluing.

4.5 Fibrewise equaliser and compatibility bundles

5.1 Bundle-valued Whitney fields
Let F−→X be a smooth real or complex vector bundle over a smooth paracompact manifold, and let
K⊆Xbe closed.
A sectionF : K−→J kF|K is called aWhitney Ck-fieldif, in every coordinate chart and local
bundle frame, its coefficient functions satisfy the classical Whitney remainder conditions of orderk. A
formal jet fieldF∞ is called asmooth Whitney fieldif each finite truncation is a WhitneyCk-field.
Coordinate and frame changes act by smooth triangular jet automorphisms, so the definitions are
intrinsic.
Proposition 5.1(Bundle-valued Whitney realisation).LetK⊆Xbe closed.
(i)Every WhitneyC k-field
F:K−→J kF|K
is realised by a section
s∈Γ k(X,F)
satisfying
jks|K =F.
(ii)Every smooth Whitney field onKis realised by a section
s∈Γ ∞(X,F)
with the prescribed full jet onK.
Proof. Choose a locally finite family of bundle-trivialising coordinate neighbourhoodsO′
i ⋐O i covering
K, and putKi =K∩ O′
i. In each frame,F|Ki is a finite-dimensional vector-valued Whitney field on the
closed Euclidean setφi(Ki); applying the scalar Whitney theorem componentwise givessi∈ Γk(Oi,F )
withj ksi|Ki =F| Ki (Whitney 1934, Fefferman 2006, Fefferman and Luli 2014).
AddO0 =X\K , sets0 = 0, and choose a partition of unity{η0,ηi} subordinate to{O0,O′
i}. The
locally finite sums =∑
iηisi is Ck. At anyx∈K , all local extensions meetingx have the samek-jet
F(x), whileη0 vanishes nearx; the Leibniz rule and∑
iηi = 1therefore give jk
xs =F(x). The C∞
statement follows from the smooth Whitney theorem and the same patching argument.

6.1 Geometric source carriers
Retain the incidence strataSλ, source-coincidence partitionsPλ, and limiting source mapsγB :Sλ−→
M1, B∈P λ, from Section 4. The finite-jet theory did not require these maps to be embeddings.
Sobolev trace theory does require additional geometric hypotheses.
Hypothesis 6.1(Embedded source-carrier hypothesis).For every pair

Different branch or stratum labels may determine the same geometric carrier. Such repetitions do
not produce independent source traces: they correspond to the same jet of the same section along the
same submanifold. The transported compatibility equations may nevertheless use that common source
trace in different ways.

6.2 Changes of trace coordinates
Derivative-trace coordinates depend on the metric, connection, tubular coordinates, and normal splitting.
Smooth choices are boundedly equivalent on compact carriers; on noncompact carriers we impose the
required uniform control.
Definition 6.8(Uniformly admissible change of trace data).Let

Proposition 6.9(Bounded equivalence of uniformly admissible trace coordinates).If two complete
incidence-trace systems are related by a uniformly admissible change at order(m,p ), then there exists a
bounded Banach-space isomorphism

Proof. For each carrier, Definition 6.8 gives a bounded lower-triangular operator on the visible Besov

bounded inverse. The finite direct sum is therefore a bounded isomorphism of the ambient trace products
and, by condition (iv), restricts toLm,p

Corollary 6.10(Compact carriers).If every carrier in ZA is compact, then any two smooth choices of
metric, connection, tubular coordinates, and normal splitting are uniformly admissible at every fixed
order(m,p ). The associated complete trace systems are boundedly equivalent whenever they encode the
same intersection compatibility conditions.
Proof. On a compact carrier, the coefficients and finitely many derivatives arising from changes of
connection, tubular coordinates, and normal splitting are uniformly bounded. Multiplication by the
leading bundle isomorphisms and their inverses is bounded on the relevant Besov spaces, and the lower-
order tangential operators have the required Besov mapping properties. The same holds for the reverse
change; finiteness of the carrier family gives a boundedly invertible direct-sum transformation.
Hence the closed Sobolev subspaces are invariant under uniformly admissible trace-coordinate
changes; no such global claim is made on noncompact carriers without uniform bounds.

The formal trace space is defined fromE∞

target. Equivalently, projection of the full formal equaliser to visible order need not equal the naive

Finite Sobolev visibility need not imply finite formal determination; the next section treats a
controlled diagonal transport class.
7 Visibility, explicit closures, and form domains
Finite Sobolev closure is governed by the trace threshold, with surviving traces constrained to the
transport equaliser.
Throughout this section, the source carriers satisfy Hypothesis 6.1, and every invocation of the
abstract Sobolev closure theorem assumes Hypothesis 6.2.

8 Scalar closure on finite graph resolutions
For finite metric-graph Hausdorff reflections, scalar closure can be proved directly from the graph
resolution: finite resolution and endpoint nondegeneracy reduce the problem to endpoint jets.
8.1 Finite smooth graph resolutions
LetΓbe a connected finite metric graph, allowing compact edges, external half-lines, parallel edges,
and loops (Berkolaiko and Kuchment 2013). Denote its vertex and edge sets byV (Γ), E (Γ). Each
edgeeis represented by an intervalIe = (0,ℓe),0<ℓ e≤∞.
43

===== PAGE 44 =====
LetE(Γ)be the finite set of edge ends. A compact edge contributes two edge ends, including two
distinct end labels when it is a loop; an external half-line contributes one finite edge end. Forϵ∈E (Γ),
writee(ϵ)∈E(Γ), v(ϵ)∈V(Γ)for the corresponding edge and incident vertex. Set
E(v) :={ϵ∈E(Γ) :v(ϵ) =v}.
Each edge end carries an outward coordinatetϵ≥ 0. At the left end of an interval this is the ordinary
coordinate; at the right end it is the distance from the right endpoint.
Definition 8.1(Finite smooth graph resolution).A finite smooth graph resolution is a triple
(M,Γ,π), π:M−→Γ,
satisfying the following conditions.
(i)Mis a second-countableT 1 smooth one-manifold without boundary, not assumed Hausdorff.
(ii)πis the Hausdorff reflection ofM.
(iii) The exceptional set
Bπ :=π−1(V(Γ))
is finite, and
π:M\B π−→Γ\V(Γ)
is a diffeomorphism.
(iv) The mapπis proper in the sense that
π−1(K)
is compact for every compactK⊆Γ.
(v) For everyp∈B π, there exist a centred chart
ϕp :U p−→(−δp,δp), ϕ p(p) = 0,
and two distinct edge ends
ϵp,+,ϵp,−∈E(π(p))
such that the two components ofUp\{p} are mapped byπ diffeomorphically onto punctured
collars of those edge ends.
Forσ∈{+1,−1}, define
κp,σ(x) :=t ϵp,σ
(
π
(
ϕ−1
p (σx)
))
,0<x<δ p.
Eachκ p,σ extends smoothly tox= 0and satisfies
κp,σ(0) = 0, κ ′
p,σ(0)>0.
(vi) Every edge end is represented by at least one local side at an exceptional point.
The Hausdorff-reflection condition is natural for the one-dimensional quotients considered here
(Haefliger and Reeb 1957, O’Connell 2023, Vlasenko and Maksymenko 2026). The nondegeneracy
condition κ′
p,σ(0) > 0implies, by the inverse-function theorem for one-sided germs, thatκp,σ has a
smooth inverse half-germ. Smoothness in the manifold coordinate is equivalent to ordinary edgewise
smoothness up to the corresponding finite edge end.
The bare resolution(M, Γ,π )does not contain the multiplicity weights used later in the quantum
theory. Those weights arise from a labelled Hausdorff presentation ofM, on which distinct resolving
representatives are counted separately even when they project to the same regular point ofM.
44

===== PAGE 45 =====
Definition 8.2(Marked analytic graph resolution).Let( M, Γ,π )be a finite smooth graph resolution.
Amarked analytic graph resolutionconsists additionally of the following data.
(i) A finite index setAand a disjoint union of Hausdorff smooth one-manifolds
~M:=
⨆
α∈A
Mα,
together with a surjective quotient map
q: ~M−→M
such that every restrictionqα :=q|Mα is a smooth local diffeomorphism onto its image. The labels
αare retained as part of the mark.
(ii) The metric structure onΓ, together with smooth positive Radon measuresµα on the resolving
sheetsM α. Writing
~µ:=
⨁
α∈A
µα,
the induced graph measure is
µΓ := (π◦q) ∗~µ.
We requireµΓ to be locally finite and absolutely continuous with respect to edge arclength on
every open graph edge.
(iii) When constant-edge models are used, the Radon–Nikodym derivative ofµΓ is constant on each
edge e; its positive value is denoted byae. More general variable densities are permitted by the
definition but are not used in the flat models of Part II.
The measure~µ is a measure on the labelled resolving presentation, not an ordinary density on the
quotient manifoldM. Two labels may contribute separately toµΓ even when their images inM agree
on an open set.
Definition 8.3(Isomorphism and projective equivalence of marked resolutions).LetR andR′ be
marked analytic graph resolutions. Amarked isomorphismconsists of a diffeomorphismF :M→M ′, a
metric-graph isomorphismΨ : Γ→ Γ′, and a diffeomorphism~F : ~M→ ~M′ carrying resolving sheets to
resolving sheets up to a permutation of their labels, such that
Ψ◦π=π ′◦F, F◦q=q ′◦ ~F, ~F∗~µ= ~µ′.
It is aprojective marked isomorphismif the last equality is replaced by~F∗~µ = c~µ′ for one constant
c>0.
Proposition 8.4(Invariance of the pushed-forward marked measure).Under a marked isomorphism,
Ψ∗µΓ =µ Γ′.
Under a projective marked isomorphism,
Ψ∗µΓ =cµ Γ′.
In the constant-edge case, the relative weight vector[a]is therefore an invariant of the projective
marked-isomorphism class.
Proof.Functoriality of pushforward gives
Ψ∗µΓ = Ψ∗(π◦q) ∗~µ= (π′◦q′◦ ~F)∗~µ.
The asserted identities follow from preservation, or common positive rescaling, of~µ. On each open
graph edge this identity is an identity of Radon–Nikodym densities, so a common rescaling changes all
edge weights by the same factor and preserves their projective class.

Bundle transport, connections, potentials, and quadratic forms are not part of the bare triple
(M, Γ,π ). When they are used below they are additional quantum data placed over a specified marked
analytic resolution.

8.4 Realisation of compatible formal jets
The classical Borel theorem realises an arbitrary formal jet at one point by a smooth function (Borel
1895, Hörmander 2003). Since the graph has finitely many edge ends, all endpoint jets can be realised
simultaneously.
Lemma 8.11(Edgewise Borel realisation).The formal endpoint trace map
Tr∞ :C∞
c,⊕(Γ)−→J ∞
Γ
is surjective.
Proof. On a compact edge, choose disjoint collars of its two endpoint labels. Apply Borel’s theorem in
each collar, multiply the resulting functions by cutoffs supported in those collars and equal to one near
the corresponding endpoint, and add them. This realises the two formal jets independently.
On an external half-line, realise the formal jet at its finite edge end and multiply by a compactly
supported cutoff equal to one near that end. Since the graph has finitely many edges, the edgewise
constructions assemble into one element ofC∞
c,⊕(Γ).
Theorem 8.12(Formal trace realisation).
Tr∞(AM) =E∞
π .
For every
r∈N 0,
Trr(AM) =J r
π.
Proof.The inclusionTr ∞(AM)⊆E ∞
π follows from Theorem 8.10.
Conversely, letξ∈E ∞
π . By Lemma 8.11, there existsu∈C ∞
c,⊕(Γ)such that Tr∞u =ξ. The formal
characterisation theorem then givesu∈A M. This proves the formal identity. Applyingprr gives
Trr(AM) = prr (E∞
π ) =J r
π.

Form∈N,1≤p<∞, setW m,p

Proof. On each compact interval or half-line,Wm,p functions have well-defined endpoint derivatives
through orderm− 1, and the corresponding trace map is bounded also forp = 1. Disjoint endpoint
collars and cutoff polynomials realise arbitrary trace data, giving surjectivity. If all endpoint traces
vanish, extension by zero across the finite endpoints belongs toWm,p; mollification, with truncation at
infinity on half-lines, approximates by smooth functions supported away from the endpoints. Taking
the finite direct sum over edges gives the kernel identity.
WriteW m,p

8.7 First-order universality

First-order scalar closure therefore depends only on the Hausdorff metric graph. The higher smooth
compatibility data of the marked resolution become visible only when derivative traces enter the Sobolev
topology.
This conclusion is specific to scalar functions and fixed trivial coefficient spaces. A nontrivially
transported vector bundle may impose a proper fibre equaliser already at order zero, as in Sections 3
and 7.

The full integer-order closure tower recovers the formal endpoint relation over the fixed graph, but
not necessarily the underlying non-Hausdorff manifold or unmarked resolution.
Remark 8.19(Scope).The results above use:
(i) finitely many edges and exceptional points;
(ii) properness of the reflection map over compact graph sets;
(iii) nondegenerate endpoint-coordinate germs;
(iv) scalar functions, or a fixed trivial finite-dimensional coefficient space with componentwise transport;
(v) integer Sobolev order and1≤p<∞.
For locally finite infinite graphs, simultaneous jet realisation and closedness require additional control
of edge lengths, vertex degrees, weights, support, and endpoint trace norms. Degenerate endpoint
coordinates may destroy ordinary edgewise Sobolev regularity before any compatibility equation is
imposed. Neither case is treated here.
Signed transport graphs organise the local endpoint relations and yield the strict inclusionsJr
π ⊊E r
π
produced by tangent-to-identity return holonomy.

The endpoint equations reduce vertexwise to formal return holonomy; this section classifies the prolon-
gation defects for tangent-to-identity return germs.

This is well defined because cancelling an edge immediately followed by its reverse removes two
orientation-reversing factors. A closed path of odd length has orientation-reversing return germ. In
particular, a transport component containing an odd cycle cannot have all its return holonomy tangent
to the identity.

9.7 Invariance
Proposition 9.18(Conjugacy invariance).Let

of fixed-jet inverse systems and identifies their formal images and prolongation quotients.

Proof. Let ~h = ψ−1hψ. If f is fixed byh, then(f◦ψ )◦~h = f◦h◦ψ = f◦ψ . Substitution by ψ
intertwines the fixed spaces, at both finite and formal order. Formal conjugation preservesord (h−id) ,
which gives the tangency-order statement.
An admissible coordinate change is a smooth or formal reparametrisation with nonzero derivative
that preserves the marked endpoint incidence data. A reversal of a centred manifold coordinate may
interchange its two side labels.
Theorem 9.19(Geometric auxiliary-choice invariance).For a fixed finite smooth graph resolution, the
componentwise fixed-jet inverse systems, prolongation quotients, orientation characters, and tangency
orders are unchanged, up to canonical isomorphism and permutation of components, under:
(i)change of root;
(ii)change of spanning tree;
(iii)reversal of a chosen transport-edge orientation;
(iv)admissible changes of centred manifold coordinates and formal endpoint-jet coordinates.
Proof. Changing the root conjugates the based holonomy group by the transport germ of a connecting
path. Changing the spanning tree alters only the chosen root parametrisation and generating loops.
Reversing an edge replaces its label by its inverse without changing the represented transport groupoid.
Under a coordinate change, the intermediate reparametrisations cancel along every closed path. The
return holonomy is therefore conjugated by the coordinate change at the root. Conjugacy preserves the
orientation sign, the linear multiplier, the fixed-jet inverse system, and the tangency order.
The same statement can be formulated at the presentation level using finite labelled groupoids
(Moerdijk and Mrčun 2003).
Proposition9.20(Labelled-groupoidpresentationinvariance).Suppose that two finite labelled transport-
groupoid presentations are isomorphic, including their objects, formal transport morphisms, and compo-
sition. Then corresponding connected components have conjugate based holonomy groups and isomorphic
fixed-jet inverse systems.
This applies, in particular, to subdivision of a presentation by composable morphisms, insertion or
deletion of identity morphisms, and insertion or deletion of redundant generators, provided that the
represented labelled groupoid is unchanged.
Proof. A labelled-groupoid isomorphism identifies closed-path morphisms after a possible change of
base object. The corresponding based holonomy groups are therefore conjugate.
These are presentation moves, not geometric insertions into a fixed resolution; a genuine elementary
endpoint transport is orientation reversing and cannot be the identity germ.
9.8 Realisation of prescribed return holonomy
Theorem 9.21(Two-cycle realisation).Let
h∈ ˆDiff1(R,0).
There exists a finite smooth graph resolution whose transport graph at one vertex has two vertices joined
by two parallel edges and whose based formal return holonomy is generated byh.
If
h̸= id,
the resulting active component has tangency order
q(h).
The same real transport acts on complex-valued scalar jets by complex linear extension.
58

===== PAGE 59 =====
Proof. By Borel realisation choose an orientation-preserving smooth germ~h with Taylor seriesh, after
shrinking so that~h is a local diffeomorphism (Borel 1895, Hörmander 2003). LetM be the topological
line with two origins, with Hausdorff reflectionπ([t,i ]) =t ontoΓ = R. Near the first origin use the
centred coordinateϕ 1([t]) =t. Near the second use
ϕ2([t]) =
{
t, t<0,
~h(t), t>0, ϕ2(02) = 0,
with domains chosen so that both charts map onto an interval about0. Their negative overlap transition
is the identity and their positive transition is~h; hence, together with the ordinary charts away from the
origins, they define a smooth second-countableT1 one-manifold without boundary. The regular locus
maps diffeomorphically toR\{ 0}, andπ is proper because the preimage of a compact set is the union
of its two compact copies.(M,Γ,π)is a finite smooth graph resolution.
Let ϵ± be the two edge ends at the graph vertex. At the first originκ1,+ =κ1,− = id, soρ1 =−id.
At the second,κ2,− = id and κ2,+ =~h−1, henceρ2 =h−1◦ (−id ). Traversing the first transport edge
and returning along the second gives
ρ1◦ρ−1
2 = (−id)◦(−id)◦h=h.
The two-parallel-edge transport component has fundamental groupZ, so its based formal holonomy
is⟨h⟩; if h̸ = id, its tangency order isq(h). Complex-valued jets are obtained by complex-linear
extension.
9.9 Sharpness and limitations
For everyq≥ 1, the germhq(T ) = T +Tq+1 satisfies q(hq) = q. Theorem 9.21 therefore shows that
every tangency order occurring in the classification is geometrically realisable.
Moreover, for everyr≥q,
Fr⟨q−1⟩
⟨hq⟩ = span{Tr}̸= 0,
whereasF r⟨q⟩
⟨hq⟩ = 0.
The invariant has the following limitations.
(i) The fixed-jet inverse system depends only onq(H). It does not detect the leading nonlinear
coefficient or any later coefficient of the return germs.
(ii) Equal tangency order does not imply formal conjugacy. The prolongation profile is not a complete
invariant of formal holonomy.
(iii) Orientation-reversing and orientation-preserving nonunit-linear return groups are excluded from
Theorem 9.15. Their finite and formal fixed spaces require a separate linear and resonant analysis.
(iv) The classification is scalar and one-dimensional. Vector-valued, fibre-coupled, and multivariable
formal holonomy require separate fixed-space calculations.
(v) The profile measures the difference between compatibility at a finite order and compatibility
through every formal order. It does not determine differential operators preserving those spaces
or their spectral and dynamical consequences.
Within the stated class of finite smooth graph resolutions, Theorem 8.12 identifies the full formal
equaliser with the formal endpoint traces of the descended smooth core, while Theorem 8.14 identifies
its finite projections with the exact integer-order Sobolev closures. The prolongation profile is therefore
an invariant of the smooth scalar graph resolution and its Sobolev closure tower, rather than merely a
count attached to an abstract formal transport system.

10.5 Geometry-selected and additional operator data
Quantum graphs admit many other self-adjoint vertex couplings (Kostrykin and Schrader 1999, Berko-
laiko and Kuchment 2013); none is assumed here.
For scalars, the resolution supplies the propagation graph and smooth core; first-order closure gives
the continuous graph domain, the marked measure supplies weights, and the positive derivative form
fixes the weighted Kirchhoff Hamiltonian without an additional vertex parameter.
Bundles can modify theH1-trace domain, while equal nonzero first-order deficiency indices admit
additional self-adjoint extension parameters. Such parameters are not part of the resolution unless
selected by further geometry.
The subsequent quantum-graph formulas are used only after identifying which reduced data the
non-Hausdorff construction selects.

12.2 Rank-one transport and classification
Equip Lk with the orientation induced by increasingx and the flat metricdx2. Since Spin(1)∼= Z2,

15 The circle withkorigins
Assume throughout this section thatk≥ 2. Fork = 1the construction is the ordinary circle and there
is no mixed origin sector.
Splitting a point on a circle differs from splitting a point on the line because the regular locus
remains connected. The local relative transport data are accompanied by one global cycle holonomy.
The resulting rank-one theory has two coherent spin sectors and a large family of mixed sectors that
collapse to the same nodal interval afterH1-completion.
15.1 Geometry and scalar reduction
Let S1
L = R/LZ and replace one pointp by k inseparable origins p1,...,p k. Denote the resulting
identity-glued manifold by Ck(L), with natural projectionq : Ck(L)−→S 1
L. Its regular locus is
Ck(L)◦∼= (0,L).
The standard exceptional charts show thatCk(L)is compact, connected, second-countable,T1, and
locally Euclidean. Pairwise inseparability of thepi gives the Hausdorff universal property ofq.
C∞(Ck(L))∼=C∞(S1
L), L 2(Ck(L))∼=L2(S1
L)
for the normalized regular-locus measure. The scalar Hamiltonian is therefore the ordinary periodic
Laplacian on the circle for every finitek≥2.
15.2 Rank-one transport and Sobolev trichotomy
Choose a spin frame on the regular interval and one near each origin. The two components of the
overlap atpi carry signsa0

Theorem 15.1(Spin classification and Sobolev reduction).The labelled spin structures are classified by

Under orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms, the classes are determined by the number of negative

TheirH 1-closures have exactly three forms:
H1

Proof. The transition ratios are invariant under local frame changes, and every choice ofη is realised.
Permuting the origins is the only orientation-preserving action on these labelled ratios, giving the orbit
count.
For a smooth regular-locus spinor,ψ(r)(L) =ηiψ(r)(0) ( r≥ 0)for every i. If allηi agree, this
gives periodic or antiperiodic full-jet matching. If two ratios differ, both endpoint jets vanish to every
order. Trace continuity and standard smooth density then give the three statedH1-closures.
The same argument for locally constantU(1)-valued transport replacesηi by phaseszi∈U (1).
If all phases agree,zi =eiθ, the closed domain is the ordinary quasiperiodic spaceH1

0(0,L ). The specifically
non-Hausdorff feature is the coexistence of incompatible local holonomies at one graph point through
inseparable origins; circle holonomy itself is standard.

16 Several split points on a circle
Several split fibres combine local nodal constraints with one global circle holonomy; any active fibre
cuts theH 1-cycle and removes that global phase.

propagation graph itself.
17 The non-Hausdorff branching line
For the branching line, the Hausdorff reflection is a star graph, so the non-Hausdorff incidence becomes
scalar channel structure rather than only exceptional-fibre multiplicity.
A second feature is supplied by measure. If the resolving sheets carry positive densitiescjdx, their
pushforward gives the common edge the sum of the sheet weights while retaining the individual weights
on the outgoing arms. The resulting balance law makes the common channel exactly reflectionless for
the resolution-induced weighted Kirchhoff Hamiltonian. The same balance makes the naturally oriented
first-order derivative symmetric, but its deficiency indices are(k− 1, 0). Genuine branching separates
second-order flux conservation from first-order self-adjoint completeness.
17.1 Geometry, Hausdorff reflection, and scalar closure
Fixk≥ 2. Let~Bk :=⨆k
j=1 Rj bethedisjointunionof k labelledcopiesoftherealline. Define Bk := ~Bk/∼,
let ϱk : ~Bk−→Bk be the quotient map, and impose the equivalence relation(x,i )∼ (x,j ) forx< 0.
No identifications are imposed forx≥0.
For x < 0, write[ x]for the common equivalence class. At x = 0there are k distinct points
bj := [(0,j)], j= 1,...,k, and forx>0write[x] j for the point on thej-th positive branch.
A neighbourhood ofbj is
Uj(ε) ={b j}∪{[x] :−ε<x<0}∪{[x] j : 0<x<ε},

away from the exceptional fibre, these charts define the standard smooth structure.
Proposition 17.1.The space Bk is a connected, second-countable,T1, smooth one-manifold. Its only
non-Hausdorff pairs are the distinct points
b1,...,b k,
which are pairwise nonseparable.

===== PAGE 74 =====
Proof. The displayed charts give local Euclideanity, and second countability follows from the finite
exceptional set and a countable basis on the regular locus. Any two branch-point neighbourhoods
contain a common negative interval, so thebj cannot be separated. Away from the exceptional fibre
the space is locally Hausdorff. Every singleton is closed: this is immediate on the regular locus, while
Bk\{bj} is open by the displayed exceptional charts. A regular point can be separated from everybj
by choosing the exceptional collar shorter than its distance from the origin, so no further non-Hausdorff
pairs occur. Connectedness follows from the common negative branch.
The regular locus isB◦

everyb j tov, and thej-th positive branch toej.
Theorem 17.2(Hausdorff reflection of the branching line).The map
π:B k−→Γk
is the Hausdorff reflection.
Proof. The map is continuous and surjective. It is also a quotient map. Indeed, letA⊆ Γk and suppose
π−1(A)is open. Openness of A away fromv follows from the edgewise homeomorphism. Ifv∈A ,
openness at the finitely many pointsbj supplies positive collar radii; their minimum gives a full star
neighbourhood ofvcontained inA.
Every continuous map fromBk to a Hausdorff space must identify the pairwise inseparable points
b1,...,b k. These are precisely the nontrivial fibres ofπ, so the required factorisation follows from the
quotient property.
Unlike the line withk origins,Γ k̸∼= R (k > 1). The same exceptional-fibre cardinality can
therefore correspond either to two scalar propagation regions, as forLk, or tok+ 1regions, as here.
A smooth scalar function onBk is represented by functionsfj∈C ∞(Rj)that agree for x < 0.

j=1ajCj = 0, which leaves a(k−1)-dimensional solution space. Hencen + =k−1.
Forψ∈ker (D∗
k +i), one hasψ′ =ψ. Square integrability forces all positive-arm components to
vanish, while the negative half-line permitsψ0(x) =C0ex. The adjoint boundary condition then gives
a0C0 = 0, soC 0 = 0. Hencen− = 0.
Corollary 17.12(No self-adjoint first-order completion).For everyk> 1, the geometrically selected
first-order operatorDk admits no self-adjoint extension in the same Hilbert space.
The deficiency space has a direct relation to the scattering decomposition. A positive-deficiency
vector is specified by(C1,...,C k)subject to ∑k
j=1ajCj = 0. After flux normalization this is precisely
the( k− 1)-dimensional arm sector orthogonal to the bright vector. The channel directions that are
dark to forward second-order transport therefore also account for the unmatched positive first-order
deficiency directions.
For comparison, the completely punctured minimal derivative on one negative andk positive half-
lines has deficiency indices(k, 1). Passing to the descended non-Hausdorff scalar core imposes the
common vertex trace and, together with the pushforward balance, reduces these to(k− 1, 0). The
descended smooth structure selects a closed symmetric operator that is not obtained as a self-adjoint
extension of the punctured derivative.
The branching line therefore exhibits two distinct consequences of the same resolution-induced
balance. The second-order Friedrichs Hamiltonian is self-adjoint and exchanges the common channel
with the bright arm mode without reflection. The naturally oriented first-order derivative is symmetric
but has a one-sided deficiency space and cannot be made self-adjoint. Both statements extend from a
1-to-ksplitter to general finitem-to-njunctions.
18 Generalm-to-nnon-Hausdorff junctions
The branching lineBk has one incoming channel andk outgoing channels. A general finite junction has
mincoming andnoutgoing propagation channels.
Unlike the1-to-k case, the channel counts do not determine the non-Hausdorff resolution. One must
also specify which incoming and outgoing branches belong to the same smooth resolving sheets. This
information is encoded by a finite bipartite multigraph.
77

===== PAGE 78 =====
The reduction separates sharply into three levels. The non-Hausdorff resolution remembers the
complete sheet incidence and the sheet densities. The resolution-induced scalarH1-theory retains only
the induced weights on the incoming and outgoing channels. The deficiency dimensions of the naturally
oriented first-order derivative retain only the channel counts:(n+,n−) = (n− 1,m− 1). Self-adjoint
first-order completion is possible precisely whenm=n.
18.1 Resolution data and Hausdorff reflection
Let I ={1,...,m}, O ={1,...,n} be the incoming and outgoing channel labels. LetS be a finite
set of resolving sheets, equipped with incidence mapsℓ :S−→I, r :S−→O . Eachα∈S connects
the incoming channelℓ(α)to the outgoing channelr(α).
Equivalently,S is the edge set of a bipartite multigraphR with vertex setI⊔O . We assume
throughout thatRis connected.

Proposition 18.1.If the incidence graphRis connected, then
BR
is a connected, second-countable,T1, locally Euclidean smooth one-manifold.
Proof. The setsUα(ε), together with ordinary intervals on the regular branches, form a smooth one-
dimensional atlas. Finiteness ofS and of the channel sets gives second countability. Regular singletons
are closed, and the complement of an originbα is open in every exceptional chart; hence the space isT1.
Finally, every sheet joins its incident incoming and outgoing branches throughbα. Connectedness of the
incidence graph propagates these joins across all channels and origins, soBR is connected.
78

===== PAGE 79 =====
Distinct origins need not be directly nonseparable. If two sheets share an incoming or outgoing
channel, however, their origins are nonseparable. Connectedness ofR propagates this relation through
chains of such incidences.
LetΓ m,n be the metric star graph with one vertexv, incoming half-linese−
1,...,e −
m, and outgoing
half-lines e+
1,...,e +
n. Define π : BR−→ Γm,n to be the identity on the regular branches and to send
everyb α tov.
Theorem 18.2(Hausdorff reflection).IfRis connected, then
π:B R−→Γm,n
is the Hausdorff reflection.
Proof. The mapπ is continuous, surjective and quotient. For the last claim, letA⊆ Γm,n with π−1(A)
open. Away from the vertex the map is an edgewise homeomorphism. Ifv∈A , openness at every
point of the finite fibre{bα} supplies collars on the incident incoming and outgoing channels. Taking
the minimum of their radii gives a star neighbourhood ofv contained inA, because every channel is
incident to a sheet in the connected graphR.
Let F : BR−→Y be continuous withY Hausdorff. Whenever two sheets share an incoming or
outgoing channel, their origins are nonseparable and hence have the same image underF. SinceR is
connected, equality propagates along a finite incidence chain, soF(bα)is independent ofα.
F is constant on the unique nontrivial fibre ofπ, and the quotient property gives the required
factorisation.
The number of origins|S| and the number of propagation channelsm +n are therefore independent
geometric quantities.

The same channel decomposition appears in both operator orders. The second-order Hamiltonian
transfers the unique bright mode and reflects the dark sectors, whereas the first-order deficiency spaces
are precisely those incoming and outgoing dark sectors. Self-adjoint first-order completion is possible
exactly when their dimensions agree.
Reversing every edge orientation exchanges the two deficiency indices. The absolute imbalance
|n+−n−| =|n−m| is therefore orientation independent, while the signed index records the oriented
excess of outgoing over incoming channels.
Form= 1, n=k, the results reduce to those of the branching line:(n +,n−) = (k−1,0).
Finite branching trees compose these junctions. Local pushforward balance then holds at every
resolution-generated vertex, allowing global propagation and first-order index calculations on the
resulting acyclic network.

19 Iterated branching and finite non-Hausdorff trees
The single-junction bright–dark law extends directly to finite acyclic networks.
For a rooted tree, resolution-induced measure is recursively additive: the weight of every parent
edge equals the sum of the weights of its children. A wave incident through the unique root channel
encounters a reflectionless channel at every successive vertex. The resulting root-to-leaf probabilities
depend only on the terminal sheet weights, not on how the branching is factored into intermediate
stages.
The first-order theory admits a similarly global description. For a finite oriented tree with positive
weights satisfying the incoming–outgoing balance condition at every internal vertex, the deficiency
indices are(n+,n−) = (Nout−1,N in−1), whereNin andNout count the incoming and outgoing external
channels. The proof requires only acyclicity, local balance, and a rank argument for the vertex equations.
19.1 Rooted resolving trees and descendant weights
LetΓbe a finite rooted metric tree with one external root edgeeroot and a finite set of external leaf
edgesL={λ 1,...,λ N}.
Orient the root edge toward the finite tree and every other edge away from the root. Every internal
vertexvhas a unique incoming parent edgep(v)and a finite set of outgoing child edgesCh(v).
A non-Hausdorff resolution of this tree is obtained by assigning one one-dimensional resolving sheet
to each terminal leaf. The sheet associated withλ follows the unique path from the root to that leaf.
Regular points belonging to sheets that traverse the same open tree edge are identified, while the
corresponding sheet origins remain distinct at branching vertices.
The resulting non-Hausdorff one-manifold will be denoted byMΓ, with Hausdorff reflectionπ :
MΓ−→Γ. Locally, a vertex withdoutgoing children is the branching modelBd.

19.3 Dark sectors and the limits of forward equivalence
At an internal vertexv, the child-channel dark space is
Dv :=
{
w∈C |Ch(v)| :⟨s v,w⟩= 0
}
,
withdimD v =|Ch(v)|−1.
A wave incident through the root never excites these sectors: at every successive vertex it arrives
through the unique parent channel and is mapped directly into the local bright state.
This is special to root incidence. A wave arriving from a terminal branch generally enters a branching
vertex through one child basis vector and therefore contains both bright and dark components. The
dark component is reflected within the child sector, allowing reverse and leaf-to-leaf scattering to probe
internal branching structure.
The associativity statement above applies to the root-incidence scattering column, not to the
complete multi-channel scattering matrix.
19.4 General balanced oriented trees
The rooted orientation is no longer assumed.
LetΓbe a connected finite metric tree with a finite set of external half-lines. Choose an orientation
on every edge. An external edge is called incoming if its orientation points from infinity toward the
finite tree and outgoing if its orientation points from the tree toward infinity.
LetE in,E out be the two external sets, and write
Nin :=|E in|, N out :=|E out|.
Assume positive edge weights ae satisfy ∑
e∈Ein(v)ae = ∑
e∈Eout(v)ae at every finite vertex v.
Resolution-induced tree weights of the type constructed above satisfy this condition.
Summing over the finite vertices cancels every finite internal edge once on each side and gives
∑
e∈Ein

∑
e∈Eout

Lemma 19.4(External channels are present in both directions).Under the positive local balance
hypothesis,
Nin≥1, N out≥1.
Proof. If the finite core consists of a single vertex, local balance with positive incident weights already
requires at least one incoming and one outgoing external edge.
Assume instead that the finite core contains an internal edge. A finite tree has a leaf vertexv in its
finite core. If no external half-line were incident atv, the unique finite edge meetingv would contribute
a strictly positive weight to exactly one side of the local balance equation, while the other side would
be zero, a contradiction. Hence at least one external half-line is present. The global identity above
equates the total incoming and outgoing external weights. Since all weights are positive, neither external
orientation class can then be empty.
OnH a =⨁

19.5 Rank of the deficiency boundary system
The global deficiency calculation reduces to the rank of these vertex relations.
Let V be the number of finite vertices. Since the finite core is a tree, it hasV− 1finite internal
edges.
Lemma 19.5(Independence of the deficiency vertex relations).For either deficiency equation
(D∗
Γ∓i)ψ= 0,
the V adjoint vertex relations are linearly independent after the non-L2 external coefficients have been
removed.
Proof. For(D∗
Γ−i )ψ = 0, only outgoing external half-lines carry nonzeroL2-modes; each internal
edge has one free coefficient. Suppose a linear combination of the vertex relations, with coefficients
λv, vanishes identically. An outgoing external coefficient occurs in only its incident vertex relation,
so λv = 0there. Lemma 19.4 supplies such a vertex. On an internal edge oriented fromu to v, the
endpoint values differ by the nonzero factore−ℓe, so cancellation forcesλu =e−ℓeλv. Connectivity of
the finite tree propagatesλ = 0to every vertex. For(D∗
Γ +i)ψ = 0, start from an incoming external
edge and use the nonzero factoreℓe. The relations are independent in both deficiency equations.
19.6 Global channel-index theorem
We can now count the deficiency dimensions.
For ker(D∗
Γ−i ), the equation isψ′ =−ψ. Incoming external half-lines carry no nonzero square-
integrable solution. Each outgoing external half-line contributes one coefficient, as does each finite
internal edge. Before imposing the vertex relations there are therefore(V−1) +Nout free coefficients.
By Lemma 19.5, theV vertex equations have full rank. Hencen+ = (V− 1) +Nout−V =Nout− 1.
Similarly, ker(D∗
Γ +i)contains one coefficient for each incoming external edge and for each finite
internal edge. Thereforen− = (V−1) +N in−V=N in−1.
Theorem 19.6(Channel-index formula for finite trees).LetΓbe a connected finite oriented metric
tree with positive weights satisfying ∑
e∈Ein(v)

∑
e∈Eout(v)
ae
at every finite vertex. Then
(n+(DΓ),n−(DΓ)) = (Nout−1, Nin−1).
n+−n− =N out−N in.
The numbers and degrees of the internal vertices, their metric separations, and the multiplicities of
the underlying resolving sheets do not enter the deficiency indices.
Corollary 19.7(Global channel-balance criterion).The resolution-induced oriented first-order operator
admits self-adjoint extensions if and only if
Nin =N out.
If
Nin =N out =r,
then
(n+,n−) = (r−1,r−1),
and the self-adjoint extensions are parametrized by
U(r−1).

===== PAGE 88 =====
For a rooted tree with one incoming root andN outgoing terminal channels,(n+,n−) = (N− 1, 0).
Every genuinely branching rooted tree has no self-adjoint extension of its geometrically selected first-order
derivative in the same Hilbert space.
The branching depth does not alter this result: a direct1-to-N junction and any finite rooted
factorisation with the same terminal channel count have identical first-order deficiency indices.
The two operator orders therefore exhibit complementary forms of compositional stability. Resolution-
induced balance makes the second-order Hamiltonian exactly reflectionless from the root, while the
first-order deficiency imbalance reduces to the asymptotic channel countNout−N in.
A tree cannot, however, produce genuine root-to-terminal path interference: each terminal channel
is reached through a unique route. Recombination provides several coherent propagation paths between
the same exterior channels. The resulting cycles convert relative path phases into observable interference.
20 Split-and-rejoin geometries and quantum interference
Recombination introduces interference between distinct routes to the same terminal channel, absent in
a tree.
We considerk resolving sheets that coincide before a splitting point, remain distinct through a
finite intermediate region, and coincide again after a recombination point. The Hausdorff reflection has
two external leads connected byk parallel internal edges. Locally, the two vertices are the balanced
branching junctions studied above. Globally, propagation along the distinct internal paths rotates the
bright mode into dark directions, producing repeated internal scattering, transmission resonances, zeros,
and compactly supported embedded eigenstates.
20.1 Geometry, weights, and the resolution-induced Hamiltonian
Takek≥ 1labelled copies R1,...,R k of the real line. Define an equivalence relation on their disjoint
union by(x,i )∼ (x,j )for x< 0 orx> 1. For0 <x< 1, the sheets remain distinct, and the
sheet origins atx= 0andx= 1remain distinct.
Denote the resulting non-Hausdorff one-manifold byMΘ,k. Its Hausdorff reflection is the metric
graphΘ k with two verticesvL,vR, one external half-line attached to each vertex, andk parallel internal
edgese 1,...,e k joiningv L tov R.
Its first Betti number isβ1(Θk) =k−1.

used below are part of the source resolution rather than data inserted only after Hausdorff reduction.

Proof. The coefficient vector(Bj)j∈R(p) lies in the kernel of the displayed two-row matrix, so the first

weights makes the nonzero row rank one in that case and two when both signs occur.
These eigenfunctions are supported entirely on the compact parallel-edge region. Since the external
leads contribute continuous spectrum[0,∞ ), their eigenvalues are embedded in the essential spectrum.
They are the standard compactly supported bound states in the continuum familiar from parallel-path
quantum graphs; here the graph and weighted coupling are selected by the non-Hausdorff resolution
(Berkolaiko and Kuchment 2013, Colin de Verdière and Truc 2018).

Interference therefore persists generically, whereas exact compact dark eigenstates require metric
commensurability.

Fork >1, the geometrically selected derivative is not self-adjoint, but its self-adjoint extensions
exist and are parametrized byU(k−1).
The equality withβ1(Θk)is a property of this particular two-vertex parallel-path family and will
not be used as a general first-order index formula for arbitrary cyclic graphs.
The result nevertheless marks the structural difference from a rooted branching tree. Splitting
alone produces the one-sided deficiency indices(k− 1, 0), whereas recombination supplies the opposite
deficiency sector and gives(k− 1,k− 1). At the same time, the parallel paths create the phase-sensitive
scattering and compact dark states derived above.
Closing the external geometry gives compact parallel-path networks in which the same bright–dark
structure is encoded entirely in the discrete spectrum.
21 Compact split-and-rejoin networks
Closing the split-and-rejoin geometry produces parallel compact paths between two vertices; interference
then appears spectrally rather than through a two-port matrix.
The compactification also gives a useful comparison for the first-order operator. The return path
restores a globally balanced orientation, but the relative parallel-path degrees of freedom remain. For
k resolved paths, the geometrically selected derivative has deficiency indices(k− 1,k− 1). The first
Betti number of the compact graph is insteadk, showing already that the equality between deficiency
dimension and cycle rank found for the open generalized theta graph is not universal.
21.1 Compact resolution and resolution-induced Hamiltonian
Takek≥ 1labelled circles S1,...,S k. On eachSj, choose two marked pointspj, q j, which divide
the circle into two open arcs. Denote byCj the arc fromqj to pj, and byIj the complementary arc
fromp j toq j.
Identify corresponding regular points ofC1,...,C k through a fixed orientation-preserving smooth
identification, while leaving the arcsI1,...,I k distinct. The marked endpoints themselves remain
distinct. Denote the resulting non-Hausdorff one-manifold byˆMΘ,k.
The pointsp1,...,p k form one pairwise nonseparable exceptional fibre, andq1,...,q k form another.
The two exceptional fibres are Hausdorff separated.
The Hausdorff reflectionˆπ : ˆMΘ,k−→ˆΘk has two verticesvL, v R, andk + 1parallel edges
connecting them: one common return edge e0 and k resolved edges e1,...,e k. Hence |E(ˆΘk)| =
k+ 1,|V( ˆΘk)|= 2, andβ 1(ˆΘk) =k.
Fork = 1, the Hausdorff reflection is an ordinary circle. Fork = 2, it is the standard three-edge
theta graph.
Equip the resolving circles with smooth positive one-dimensional metrics that agree under the
identification of the common arcsC1,...,C k. Let ℓ0 > 0be the resulting common length of the
identified return arc and letℓj > 0, j = 1,...,k , be the length of the complementary resolved arc
Ij on thej-th circle. These lengths therefore descend from the source metrics onˆMΘ,k.

21.4 Zero-vertex dark states and commensurability
The all-energy system contains a distinguished class withU =V = 0. Such an eigenfunction can be
nonzero only on edges satisfyingpℓe∈πZ.
Define
Rc(p) :={e∈{0,...,k}:pℓ e∈πZ},

Proof. The zero-vertex coefficient vector belongs to the kernel of the displayed matrix. Rank–nullity
gives the first formula. If the resonant set is nonempty and allσe agree, the two nonzero rows are
proportional; if both signs occur, positivity of the weights makes them independent.

===== PAGE 97 =====
The theorem gives only the zero-vertex part of a pole eigenspace; Proposition 21.2 also allows
solutions with nonzero endpoint values.
Simultaneous Dirichlet resonance is therefore not synonymous with a purely dark eigenvalue.
If the lengths are pairwise rationally incommensurate, no two distinct edges can resonate simultane-
ously at positive momentum. A single resonant edge cannot satisfy the first dark Kirchhoff condition
with nonzero coefficient. Hence the zero-vertex dark sector is absent in that case.

21.6 Weyl asymptotics and relation to the open interferometer

Weyl term.

Cutting the common return edge and replacing the cut directions by leads recovers the open
parallel-path core of Section 20; compact phase quantization is then replaced by scattering.
This comparison is structural rather than an operator identification, because compact closure and
asymptotic opening impose different global problems.

Part III
Unitary gluing and invariant-sector scattering
For finite-rank gluing, the common fixed space is the transmitting sector. Part III identifies this
space with compact-group invariants and studies the number of relative gluings required for invariant
completion.

26.2 Completion and topological generation
IfΓ = ⟨g1,...,g m⟩, then⋂m
a=1 ker(ρ(ga)−I ) =V Γ. For a continuous representation, density ofΓinG

Proposition 26.6(Comparison with topological generation).Let G be a topologically finitely generated
compact group and letρ:G→GL(V)be a continuous finite-dimensional representation. Then
ν(ρ)≤d top(ρ(G))≤d top(G).
Proof. SinceG is compact,ρ(G)is compact. A dense generatingm-tuple inρ(G), withm =dtop(ρ(G)),

Corollary 26.7(Two-element completion for compact semisimple groups).LetG be a connected compact
semisimple Lie group and letρ :G→U (V )be a finite-dimensional continuous unitary representation.
Then
ν(ρ)≤2.
IfGis nontrivial, then
dtop(G) = 2.
ForG =SU (N),N≥ 2, L3 has enough relative-gluing slots to realiseVSU(N) for every finite-dimensional
continuous unitary representation.
Proof. If G is trivial, thenν(ρ) = 0. Assume thatG is nontrivial. The dense-two-generation theorem
for connected semisimple real Lie groups (Breuillard and Gelander 2003, 2026), applied toG as a dense
subgroup of itself, suppliesg1,g 2∈G with dense generated subgroup. HenceVg1∩V g2 =VG, which
givesν(ρ)≤ 2. A nontrivial connected compact semisimple group is non-Abelian, while the closure of a
cyclic subgroup is abelian, so one element cannot generateGdensely.
For nontrivial connected compact semisimpleG, dense generating pairs form a Zariski-open dense
subset ofG 2 (Chirvasitu 2021).
Both inequalitiesν(ρ)≤d top(ρ(G))≤d top(G)can be strict. The first can be strict because a proper
subgroup ofρ(G)may have the same fixed space asρ(G); the second can be strict whenρ has nontrivial
kernel or otherwise lowers the topological generator number of the image.

every t∈T fixes V0. For regular t, its centralizer isT; any further element that removes residual
zero-weight vectors must therefore lie outside the centralizer oft.

28SU(N)tensor invariants and transmission multiplicities
Let F := CN denote the defining representation ofSU (N), and letF denote its complex-conjugate
representation. We consider the mixed tensor spaces
Vp,q :=F ⊗p⊗F⊗q, p,q≥0.(2)
The invariant sectors of these representations are standard objects of classical invariant theory and
Schur–Weyl duality (Weyl 1997, Fulton and Harris 1991, Goodman and Wallach 2009, Howe 1989). For
invariant-complete gluing, Theorem 23.2 identifies these invariant multiplicities with transmission ranks.
28.1 The center obstruction
The center ofSU(N)is

{
ζIN :ζ N = 1
}

The central elementζIN acts onVp,q by the scalar
ζp−q.(3)
Proposition 28.1(Center selection rule).For

one has
VSU(N)

The congruence determines existence but not invariant multiplicity.

28.2 Balanced tensor powers
For the balanced sectorsVm,m =F ⊗m⊗F⊗m, the central action is trivial.
The Hermitian structure onF identifiesF∼=F∗ asSU (N)-representations. Hence there is a natural
equivariant identification
F⊗m⊗F⊗m∼= End(F⊗m),(4)
under which theSU(N)action becomes conjugation. Therefore
(
F⊗m⊗F⊗m)SU(N) ∼= EndSU(N) (F⊗m).(5)
In the balanced tensor representation the centralU(1)⊂U (N)acts trivially. Since every element of
U(N)can be written as a central phase times an element ofSU (N), theSU (N)- andU(N)-invariant
subspaces ofVm,m coincide. Schur–Weyl duality then identifies the commutant of theU(N)-action on
F⊗m with the image of the place-permutation action of the symmetric groupSm (Fulton and Harris
1991, Goodman and Wallach 2009).
Forσ∈S m, letPσ :F ⊗m−→F⊗m be the permutation operator
Pσ(v1⊗···⊗v m) =v σ−1(1)⊗···⊗v σ−1(m).(6)
Theorem 28.3(Balanced invariant multiplicity).LetN≥m. Then
(
F⊗m⊗F⊗m)SU(N) ∼= span{Pσ :σ∈S m},(7)
and the operatorsPσ are linearly independent. Hence
dim

=m!.(8)
Proof. By (5), the invariant space is the commutant of the diagonalSU (N)action on F⊗m. As observed
above, this commutant is the same as that forU(N). Schur–Weyl duality identifies it with the image of
the group algebraC[Sm]under the permutation actionσ↦→Pσ.
It remains to show that them!permutation operators are linearly independent whenN≥m . Choose
linearly independent basis vectorse1,...,e m∈F . The vectors Pσ(e1⊗···⊗e m), σ∈S m, are
the m!distinct tensors obtained by permuting them distinct basis labels and are therefore linearly
independent. If∑

C[Sm]has dimensionm!.
The equality withm!is the stable-range statementN≥m ; form>N , permutation contractions
satisfy linear relations.

30.4 One- and two-gluing optima
Proposition 29.6 givesr1(Vquart) = 15, r 2(Vquart) = 2. A generic torus element realises the one-gluing

30.6 Relation to tetraquark color space
In particle-physics notation,
Vquart =3⊗3⊗ 3⊗ 3.
The representation is the color space for two fundamental and two antifundamental color factors,
and its two-dimensional invariant subspace is the standard two-singlet sector used in tetraquark color
decompositions(Slansky1981,ParkandLee2014). Thepresentmodelcontainsasinglespatialcoordinate
and no Yang–Mills field, gluon exchange, confinement, spin, flavor, or four-body configuration-space
dynamics. The tetraquark connection is therefore confined to the internalSU (3)representation and its
invariant projector.

Theoriginthresholdforinvariantcompletionis kinv(ρ) = max{2,ν (ρ)+1}. Theintrinsicmatrix-group

For a nontrivial connected compact semisimple groupG,dtop(G) = 2, hence every finite-dimensional
continuous unitary representation satisfiesdtop(ρ(G))≤ 2. Accordingly, L3 suffices for dense generation
of the matrix image and for invariant completion. Individual representations can satisfyν(ρ) = 1, in
which case invariant completion is already realizable onL2.

32 Representation-theoretic input and model dependence
The representation-theoretic inputs above–Haar averaging, infinitesimal invariants, theSU (N)center
rule, Schur–Weyl duality, and low-degree singlet multiplicities–are standard (Hall 2015, Bröcker and
tom Dieck 1985, Weyl 1997, Fulton and Harris 1991, Goodman and Wallach 2009, Howe 1989); common
fixed-space questions are likewise classical (Bernik et al. 2005).
The finite-origin model realises the chain from a finite gluing tuple to its common fixed space, then
byH1-closure to the transmitting sector of the flat Hamiltonian. Thek− 1relative transition slots
bound how far this chain can proceed onLk.
Once the free–Dirichlet boundary decomposition has been obtained, its two-lead scattering matrix
is standard point-interaction and quantum-graph scattering theory (Kostrykin and Schrader 1999,
Berkolaiko and Kuchment 2013, Albeverio et al. 2005). The non-Hausdorff step is the derivation of that
decomposition from bundle compatibility and Sobolev trace closure.
The lawT =PVW, R =−(I−P VW)is specific to the collapse measure, locally constant unitary
gluing, and flat Dirichlet form; other connections, potentials, or defect interactions can retain further
representation data.
ForF⊗2⊗F⊗2, F = C3, the invariant sector is the standard two-dimensional singlet color
space used in tetraquark decompositions (Slansky 1981, Park and Lee 2014). No claim is made about
four-body dynamics or QCD phenomenology.
Extensions that retain holonomy, resolve invariant multiplicity spaces, or introduce genuine multi-
particle spatial degrees of freedom require additional geometric or operator-theoretic structure.
Part IV compares the information retained by the propagation graph, measure, bundle domain, and
operator realisation.
116

===== PAGE 117 =====
Part IV
Reduction and quantum detectability
33 Weighted Hausdorff reduction: topology and measure
Exceptional-fibre multiplicity is not itself a scalar propagation invariant: the boundaryless first-order
reduction retains the metric Hausdorff graph and projective edge weights, while boundary variants also
retain the closed scalar trace domain.
33.1 Propagation graph and distinct multiplicities
Let q :X−→ Γbe a finite graph resolution. For a vertexv over whichq has a nontrivial fibre, define
κ(v) :=|q−1(v)| and letd(v)be the number of incident graph germs. For a noncompact graph write
Next for the number of external half-lines, and for a connected finite graph setβ1(Γ) =|E|−|V|+ 1.
Exceptional multiplicity and graph valence are distinct. If the local quotient is one graph vertex,
µ(v) = |Sv| whereas d(v) = |P−(v)| +|P+(v)|; channel multiplicity is controlled by punctured-side
partitions. Lk has( µ,d ) = (k, 2), Bk has( k,k + 1), and a two-leadk-arm theta graph hasNext = 2and
β1 =k−1.
Proposition 33.1(Exceptional multiplicity does not determine channels).Within the multiple-origin
lines, intervals, and circles considered in Part II, increasing the multiplicity of a fixed exceptional fibre
does not increase the local valence of the Hausdorff propagation graph. In the branching resolutions of
Part II, the resolving incidence can change that valence.
33.2 Weighted reduction theorem
LetAX be the descended scalar smooth core and writeVX :=AX

trace domain containingC∞
c (Γ\V (Γ)). For the boundaryless finite smooth graph resolutions of Section 8,
Corollary 8.15 givesVX =H1
cont(Γ). Boundary models may impose additional ordinary endpoint trace
conditions.
Let themarked analyticpresentation ofDefinition 8.2 carry measure~µ, and assume thatµΓ = (π◦q)∗~µ
has constant positive edge densitiesae>0. SetH Γ,a =⨁
eL2(e,aedx)and define

Theorem 33.2(Weighted Hausdorff reduction).Under these hypotheses, the resolution-induced scalar
kinetic form and its associated Friedrichs Hamiltonian are completely determined by the reduced datum
(Γ,a,V X).
IfVX =H1
cont(Γ), the trace-domain entry is universal and the reduced datum simplifies to the weighted
metric graph(Γ,a); the associated operator is then the weighted Kirchhoff Laplacian of Theorem 10.2.
Proof. The completed scalar core fixes the closed form domainVX. The marked resolving-presentation
measure fixes the Hilbert norm and derivative form through its pushforwardµΓ. The representation
theorem for closed nonnegative forms therefore determines the associated self-adjoint operator from
(Γ, a,VX). WhenVX =H1
cont(Γ), integration by parts gives exactly the weighted Kirchhoff realisation of
Theorem 10.2. No higher scalar smooth data enter this second-order operator once the reduced datum
has been fixed.
For the boundary variant of Section 14, the ordinary endpoint conditions are encoded inVX; they
are not recoverable from(Γ,[a])alone.
A common positive rescaling of the weights is immaterial.

Corollary 33.3(Projective weight invariance).Fix the metric graph and closed trace domainVX. If

Proof. Multiplication by√c gives a unitary map fromL2(Γ,c adx)to L2(Γ, adx), and the form is

For branching, topology fixes the arms while the projective weights fix the bright-mode probabilities

33.3 Scalar equivalence and information loss
Corollary 33.4(ScalarH 1-equivalence).Let
X1−→Γ 1, X 2−→Γ 2
satisfy the hypotheses of Theorem 33.2, with closed trace domainsV1 andV2. If a metric-graph
isomorphism
F: Γ 1−→Γ 2
preserves the projective edge weights and carriesV1 ontoV2, then the resulting scalar closed forms and
Friedrichs Hamiltonians are unitarily equivalent.
Information loss is explicit: Lk forgets origin multiplicity, while anm-to-n junction reduces its
incidence matrixC = (Cij)to row and column sums. Equal marginals therefore give the same scalar
vertex scattering, although the reduced graph still retains valence, lengths, cycles, and projective weights.
Observable equivalence is coarser still. Root incidence on a resolving tree suppresses the internal
hierarchy, whereas reverse incidence can access dark sectors; recombination restores sensitivity through
J(p),K (p), and compactification transfers the same path information to spectral quantization. Even
then, leading Weyl asymptotics retain only total metric length.
Recombination restores direct phase sensitivity to internal path data. For the generalized theta
graph, the external scattering law depends onJ(p) =∑
jaj csc(pℓj), K (p) =∑
jaj cot(pℓj). Cycles
can restore observable sensitivity to internal metric structure that a forward experiment on an acyclic
network loses.
33.4 Limits of the scalar invariant
The weighted graph alone is complete only forH1
cont(Γ). In general the reduced scalar datum is
(Γ, [a],VX), withVX recording additional closed trace conditions; this determines the flat scalar form
and Friedrichs operator but not bundle or higher-smooth data.
The same(Γ, [a])can therefore support different scalar or bundle trace domains, while the oriented
first-order derivative retains further orientation and deficiency data.
34 Bundle domains, holonomy, and smooth resolution
The weighted scalar graph does not determine bundle domains or higher smooth compatibility: unitary
transport changes value traces, whereas branch reparametrizations may enter only higher jets.

===== PAGE 119 =====
34.1 Rank-one transport and nodal collapse
Let q :X−→ Γbe a finite graph resolution with projective edge weights[a], and letE−→X be a
rank-one Hermitian bundle with locally constant unitary transition functions. After choosing frames
on the regular graph edges, sections are represented by scalar edge functions. The bundle changes the
admissible endpoint relations, not the local differential expressions.
Consider a multiple-origin defect joining the same two regular edge germs, with relative transports
z1,...,z k∈U(1). The endpoint equaliser is

Proposition 34.1(Rank-one endpoint dichotomy).If
z1 =···=z k =:z,
then

E0

Proof. The coherent case is immediate. Ifzi̸=zj, thenziψ+ =ψ− =zjψ+ forces ψ+ = 0, hence also
ψ− = 0.
In the identity-glued models, the same fibre relation holds at every smooth derivative order. Incom-
patible transport therefore forces a flat smooth core but only a node afterH1-closure. This collapses
the2 k−1 labelled spin structures onLk to twoH1-domains, and with several split points only the active
defect set survives.
34.2 Cycle holonomy and analytic cutting
A cycle can retain coherent bundle information that no individual local equaliser detects. If all local
transports are coherent, local frame changes remove them except for their productZ = eiθ∈U (1).
On a circle of lengthL, the resulting domain isH1
Z(0,L ) ={ψ∈H 1(0,L ) :ψ(L) =Zψ(0)}, and the
Friedrichs operator satisfies the corresponding derivative relationψ′(L) = Zψ′(0). Its spectrum is
λn(Z) =

, n∈Z.
Theorem 34.2(Holonomy under nodal cutting).For the rank-one circle resolutions of Sections 15
and 16, coherentU(1)holonomy affects the resultingH1-theory precisely while the corresponding cycle
remains uncut by an active nodal defect. Once such a defect imposes zero trace, the global holonomy no
longer affects the Friedrichs Hamiltonian.
Proof. With no active defect, local frame changes reduce the coherent transports to their productZ,
giving the quasiperiodic trace relation and the spectrum displayed above. An active defect imposes zero
trace and decomposes the form domain into Dirichlet path components. Constant phase changes on
those components remove every remaining coherent local transport, so the closed form and its Friedrichs
operator are independent ofZ.
34.3 Bundle classes, Hamiltonians, and extension data
Proposition 34.3(Hamiltonian equivalence criterion).Let E1,E 2→X be rank-one bundle sectors
over the same weighted propagation graph. If their regular-locus Hilbert identifications agree and
CE1

=CE2
H1

then their resulting kinetic forms and Friedrichs Hamiltonians coincide under that identification.
119

===== PAGE 120 =====
Proof. The two forms have the same edge weights, differential expression, and closed form domain.
Their associated self-adjoint operators are therefore identical.
The same statement applies to the geometrically selected first-order operator once an orientation is
fixed. However, the first-order and second-order theories ask different questions. The positive form has
a unique self-adjoint Friedrichs realisation; the edgewise derivative may be self-adjoint, may admit a
family of self-adjoint extensions, or may have unequal deficiency indices and no self-adjoint extension.
34.4 Smooth resolution beyondH 1
The smooth resolution can contain information that is absent even from the bundle-sensitiveH1-
domain. Near a resolved vertex, two branch coordinates may be related by a smooth germh(t) =
λt +c2t2 +c3t3 +···, λ̸ = 0. Derivative transport depends on the jet ofh:( f◦h )′(0) =h′(0)f′(0),
(f◦h )′′(0) =h′(0)2f′′(0) +h′′(0)f′(0). Value matching does not determine higher smooth compatibility.

Theorem 34.4(Second-order smooth-resolution blindness).Let
πi :M i−→Γ, i= 1,2,
be scalar graph resolutions satisfying first-order scalar universality and inducing the same projective edge
weights and edge metrics. Then their resolution-induced scalar kinetic forms and weighted Kirchhoff
Hamiltonians are unitarily equivalent. Differences carried solely by higher transported endpoint jets are
invisible to this second-order Friedrichs theory.
Proof. First-order scalar universality identifies both closed form domains withH1
cont(Γ). Equality of the
projective weights gives, after the common unitary normalization of Corollary 33.3, the same Hilbert
norm and kinetic form. The associated self-adjoint operators are therefore unitarily equivalent. Higher
endpoint-jet relations do not enter either closed form.
Variationally, constraints lost in the closedH1-form domain are not restored merely because the
associated operator acts edgewise onH2.

Higher Sobolev orders successively expose endpoint jets: in one dimensionu(j)(v)is continuous onWm,p
whenj <m− 1/p. A tangent-to-identity return germh(t) =t +atq+1 +O(tq+2)acts trivially on values
but can alter these higher traces, and finite compatibility may still fail formal prolongation through
